Camp General Emilio Aguinaldo, Quezon City

Dear Secretary Teodoro:

## Consolidated Report on the Audit of the Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Funds For the Calendar Year Ended 2024

## I. Authority, Objectives, and Scope of Audit

## A. Authority

1. Pursuant to Section 2, Article IX-D of the 1987 Philippine Constitution and Section 43 of the Government Auditing Code of the Philippines<sup>1</sup>, Audit Teams of various National Government Agencies (NGAs), Government-Owned and/or Controlled Corporations (GOCCs), and Local Government Units (LGUs) have audited the Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Funds (DRRMF) as part of their regular audit.  
2. A Consolidated Report has been prepared in compliance with Section VII, Par. 9, of COA Memorandum No. 2014-009, dated August 28, 2014.

## B. Objectives

3. This report aims to improve the availability, reliability, and quality of financial information on disaster funds, promote transparency, enhance accountability, and mitigate potential risks in disaster-related transactions.

## C. Scope

4. The report covers the audit results on the receipts and utilizations of the DRRMF for the Calendar Year 2024 as provided under Republic Act (RA) No. 10121, otherwise known as the Philippine Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Act of 2010.  
5. The Financial Highlights of this report were based on the summary of releases taken from the Department of Budget and Management (DBM) website and other financial data submitted by the Audit Teams of 167 NGAs, 91 GOCCs, and 1,651 LGUs.  
Moreover, the consolidated significant audit observations and recommendations were lifted from the Annual Audit Reports (AARs)<sup>2</sup> of 10 NGAs, 6 GOCCs, along with the region-wide consolidated significant audit observations and recommendations of 54 provinces, 84 cities, and 739 municipalities submitted by the Office of the Assistant Commissioner, Local Government Audit Sector (OAC-LGAS) of this Commission.  
7. Lastly, the Status of Implementation of Prior Years’ Audit Recommendations was gathered from the AARs of 8 NGAs and 4 GOCCs, along with the 812 LGUs included in the region-wide consolidated significant audit observations and recommendations submitted by the OAC-LGAS. The details are presented in Annex A.

## II. Introduction

8. Due to the country’s susceptibility to natural and human-induced disasters, the government has made continuous efforts in the past several years to build people’s capacities and resilience to disasters.  
9. This eventually led to the enactment of RA No. 10121, otherwise known as the Philippine Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Act of 2010, which paved the way for a paradigm shift in the country’s disaster management system from one primarily focused on response and preparedness for response to one that is more focused on reducing and managing disaster risks<sup>3</sup>.  
10. This national policy transformed and reformed how the Philippines deals with disasters. It strongly recognized that risks need not become disasters and that the impacts of disasters can be reduced by addressing the underlying causes of risks. It emphasized the importance of strengthening people’s capacity to absorb shocks and stresses, maintaining basic functions during disasters, and recovering better from disasters. It likewise recognized some long-standing good practices at the local level and institutionalized them for upscaling.  
11. To implement the provisions of RA No. 10121, the National Disaster Risk Reduction Management Council (NDRRMC) was created and empowered by relevant agencies involved in DRRM by virtue of their respective mandates.  
12. The NDRRMC is composed of 44 members, headed by the Secretary of the Department of National Defense (DND) with four Vice-Chairpersons, namely: the Secretary of the Department of Science and Technology (DOST) for Disaster Prevention and Mitigation, the Secretary of the Department of the Interior and Local Government (DILG) for Disaster Preparedness, the Secretary of the Department of Social Welfare and Development (DSWD) for Disaster Response, and the Secretary of the Department of Economy, Planning and Development (DEPDev) for Disaster Rehabilitation and Recovery. The structure of the National Council is replicated in all regions. Every province, city, municipality, and barangay has its council/ committee, formed and chaired by its respective Local Chief Executives (LCEs).  
13. The Office of Civil Defense (OCD) is the NDRRMC's implementing arm and serves as the Council's Secretariat.  
14. The NDRRMC is also supported by various Regional Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Councils (RDRRMC) and Local Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Councils (LDRRMC) nationwide, also known as its Networks.  
15. The powers and functions of the NDRRMC and its Networks include the following:

a. The NDRRMC is empowered with policy-making, coordination, integration, supervision, monitoring, and evaluation functions;  
b. The OCD has the primary mission of administering a comprehensive national civil defense and DRRM program by providing leadership in the continuous development of strategic and systematic approaches, as well as measures to reduce the vulnerabilities and risks to hazards and manage the consequences of disasters;  
c. The RDRRMC is tasked to coordinate, integrate, supervise, and evaluate the activities of the LDRRMC. It is responsible for ensuring disaster-sensitive regional development plans. In case of emergencies, it shall convene the different regional line agencies and concerned institutions and authorities; and

d. The LDRRMC is responsible to approve, monitor and evaluate the implementation of the DRRM plan and regularly review and test the plan consistent with other national and local planning programs; to ensure the integration of disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation into local development plans, programs and budgets as a strategy in sustainable development and poverty reduction; to recommend the implementation of forced or pre-emptive evacuation of residents, if necessary; and to convene the local council once every three months or as required.

16. RA No. 10121 also enumerated various sources of funds to be used by all government agencies and instrumentalities to implement the identified DRRM programs, activities, and projects (PAPs).  
17. Emergency procedures are usually introduced in the use of disaster funds. As such, procedural controls are frequently bypassed to save lives and mitigate losses<sup>4</sup>. Under these conditions, the Audit Teams and the people who have fiscal responsibility over the said funds face challenges in handling and managing risks inherent in disasterrelated activities<sup>5</sup>.  
18. COA’s goals to improve the availability, reliability, and quality of financial information on disaster funds, make it more transparent, enhance accountability, and mitigate potential risks in disaster-related transactions led to the promulgation of various circulars and memoranda prescribing the accounting and reporting guidelines on the receipt and utilization of DRRMF.  
19. These guidelines recognize that transparency and accountability in the use of DRRMF and any disaster relief aid/donations have considerable importance to the public, donors, bilateral and multilateral organizations, and other stakeholders.

## III. Financial and Operational Highlights

20. The Financial Highlights of this report include a summary of releases taken from the DBM website and other financial data submitted by the Audit Teams of 167 NGAs, 91 GOCCs, and 1,651 LGUs. The details are presented in Annex B.  
21. On the other hand, the Operational Highlights of this Report were taken from the Accomplishment Report developed by the OCD as the executive arm and secretariat of the NDRRMC, cognizant of the Council’s mandated functions encompassing policy-making, coordination, integration, supervision, monitoring, and evaluation under Section 6 of RA No. 10121.

## A. Financial Highlights

22. To achieve the vision of a safer, adaptive, and disaster-resilient Filipino communities toward sustainable development, RA No. 10121 established various sources of funds (herein referred to as the DRRMF) to support and implement the identified DRRM PAPs, to wit:

a. A disaster fund on the national level, herein referred to as National Disaster Risk Reduction Management Fund (NDRRMF), shall be used for disaster risk reduction or mitigation, prevention, and preparedness activities. It can also be utilized for relief, recovery, and reconstruction efforts that address the impact of calamities that occurred during the budget year or disasters that happened two years prior to the budget year<sup>6</sup>;  
b. Quick Response Fund (QRF) is also appropriated to serve as a stand-by fund to be used for relief and rehabilitation programs and projects so that the situation and living conditions of people in communities or areas stricken by disasters, calamities, epidemics, or complex emergencies, may be normalized as quickly as possible<sup>7</sup>;  
c. All departments, bureaus, offices, and agencies of the government are also authorized to use a portion of their appropriations to implement projects designed to address DRRM activities in accordance with the guidelines issued by the NDRRMC and DBM<sup>8</sup>;  
d. LGUs are likewise mandated to fund their DRRM activities from the following sources (herein referred to as LDRRMF):

● At least five percent of their estimated revenue from regular sources to support DRRM activities;  
● Unexpended balance of the LDRRMF in the preceding years within the fiveyear validity period of the Special Trust Fund (STF);  
● Fund transferred from the NDRRMF upon the approval of the President; and  
● Fund received from other LGUs and other sources;

e. All agencies are also allowed to receive financial aid or donations, both in cash and in kind, from local and foreign sources.

23. The NDRRMF appropriated during the year is composed of the following:

a. Appropriations under Fiscal Year (FY) 2024 General Appropriations Act<sup>9</sup> (GAA) amounting to ₱20.50 billion, herein referred to as NDRRMF; and  
b. Continuing Appropriations (ConAp) under FY 2023 GAA<sup>10</sup> amounting to ₱2.24 billion for the implementation of the prior year’s NDRRMF.

24. Out of the total appropriated NDRRMF totaling ₱22.74 billion, DBM released ₱22.60 billion during the year.

25. QRF appropriations totaling ₱7.93 billion were fully released through the 2024 GAA of nine core agencies, namely: Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP), Department of Agriculture (DA), Department of Education (DepEd), Department of Health (DOH), Department of Public Works and Highways (DPWH), DSWD, Philippine Coast Guard (PCG), Philippine National Police (PNP), and OCD.

26. Moreover, various NGAs and GOCCs reported allocation totaling ₱6.65 billion from their regular budget to implement various DRRM PAPs.

27. Meanwhile, 1,566 LGUs allocated an aggregate amount of ₱49.14 billion for its LDRRMF for CY 2024.

28. Various cash donations totaling ₱5.06 billion were also reported during the year.

29. The funds above totaling ₱91.38 billion, along with the adjusted beginning balance of the DRRMF totaling ₱84.41 billion, comprise the total fund made available for use during the year in the amount of ₱175.79 billion.

30. Out of the funds available for the year, ₱98.78 billion was utilized/transferred to other agencies while the amount of ₱251.46 million was reverted to the Bureau of Treasury (BTr), leaving a balance of ₱76.76 billion as of report date. A summary of fund receipts, utilizations, and balances is presented in Table 1.

## A.1. Summary of Receipts, Utilizations, and Balances of NDRRMF

## • Beginning Balance, Receipts, and Available Funds for the Year

31. As shown in Table 1, various NGAs and GOCCs still have prior years’ unutilized funds totaling ₱22.42 billion, which are available for use during the year. DBM also released allotments for the NDRRMF for CY 2024 totaling ₱22.60 billion to eight NGAs and three GOCCs. As such, a total fund of ₱45.02 billion is made available for use.  
32. Out of the total allotments released by DBM to various NGAs and GOCCs, DPWH received the highest amount of ₱12.09 billion, followed by the DSWD with a total allotment of ₱8.14 billion, OCD with ₱557 million, and DA with ₱500 million. Graphical representation is shown in Figure 1.

## A.2. Summary of Receipts, Utilization, and Balances of QRF

36. At the beginning of the year, eight out of 10 agencies still have unutilized QRF amounting to ₱1.43 billion. This amount, along with the QRF released during the year totaling ₱7.93 billion, comprised the total available funds of ₱9.36 billion.  
37. Out of the total available funds, these agencies utilized a total amount of ₱5.85 billion or 62.51 percent. In comparison, the amount of ₱46.96 million was reverted to the BTr, leaving a balance of ₱3.46 billion at year-end. ## B. Operational Highlights

## National Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Plan (NDRRMP)

47. To implement RA No. 10121, the DRRM Framework was developed and approved on June 16, 2011 to provide a comprehensive, all-hazards, multi-sectoral, interagency and community-based approach to DRRM. Thereafter, OCD led the development of the NDRRMP 2011-2028.  
48. The NDRRMP serves as a roadmap towards the realization of DRRM and sets out goals and specific objectives for reducing disaster risks together with related actions to accomplish these objectives.  
49. To ensure its relevance and responsiveness to the evolving risks the country faces, RA No. 10121 mandated the conduct of reviews to assess the progress on the DRRM Framework and NDRRMP.  
50. As prescribed under the law, a Sunset Review was conducted to provide a systematic evaluation of the accomplishments and impact of the Act as well as the performance and organizational structure of its implementing agencies.  
51. Relative thereto, the updated NDRRMP 2020-2030 was developed and duly approved by the Council to help the country move towards its vision of resilience, as stated in its framework.  
52. The updated NDRRMP, consisting of 23 outcomes, 50 outputs and 206 activities, is guided by various considerations, including but not limited to the following:  
a. The four thematic areas were retained, but the outcomes, outputs, and activities were revised based on the learnings for the past eight years of implementation;  
b. The four thematic areas are still to be steered by the four vice chairpersons of the NDRRMC;  
c. The updated timeline is attuned to the national development planning cycles, and the NDRRMP will use a common timeline for all the activities;  
d. Risk reduction strategies and plans, across different timescales and landscapes, with targets, indicators, and time frames, aimed at preventing the creation of risk, the reduction of existing risk, and the strengthening of economic, social, health, and environmental resilience were adopted; and  
e. The need to invest in the health of all Filipinos as demonstrated by the COVID-19 pandemic was considered. This is done by prioritizing health and wealth-being in DRRM actions.

53. The reported accomplishments of various member-agencies for the four thematic areas are as follows:

<table><tr><td colspan="3">Thematic Area I: Disaster Prevention and Mitigation</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3">GOAL:➢ Address current and reduce future risks of communities and government through mainstreaming integrated risk management into science, policy and practice.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3">OBJECTIVES:➢ Improve access, understanding, and use of updated risk information, DRR-related statistics, and research;➢ Apply integrated risk management assessment tools;➢ Implement risk-centered national, sub-national and sectoral policies, plans and budgets;➢ Institutionalize timely, responsive, context- and culture-specific early warning systems (EWS);➢ Access to effective, responsive and inclusive risk financing and insurance mechanisms.➢ Improve and protect ecosystem integrity;➢ Build resilience of livelihoods and businesses; and➢ Disaster-resilient human settlements</td></tr><tr><td>OUTCOME 1:</td><td colspan="2">Improved access, understanding and use of updated risk information and research</td></tr><tr><td>Lead Agency:</td><td colspan="2">DOST</td></tr><tr><td>Implementing Partners:</td><td colspan="2">OCD, DILG, DA, DOH, DENR, DENR-MGB, DENR-NAMRIA, CCC, DICT, DOSTPHIVOLCS, DOST-PAGASA, DOST-PNRI, DSWD, DOE, DTI, DepEd, DPWH, DOT, DEPDev, OPAPP, PCOO, NAPC-VDC, LPP, LCP, LMP, ULAP, LMB, PRC, CSOs, Private Sector, LGUs, and DRRMCsOther Agencies: PSA, NCDA, Development Partners, and other agencies/Institutions/Organizations deemed necessary</td></tr><tr><td>Indicator</td><td>Reporting Agency</td><td>Accomplishment</td></tr><tr><td>1. ## Thematic Area II: Disaster Preparedness

## GOAL:

⮚ Establish and strengthen capacities of governments, communities, CSOs, and private sector to anticipate, cope, and recover from the adverse impacts of hazards and potential cascading disasters, and minimize losses and disruption of daily life.

## OBJECTIVES:

⮚ Increase the level of awareness and understanding of governments and communities of hazards, exposure, and vulnerabilities;  
⮚ Equip governments, institutions, communities, families, and individuals with the necessary skills to respond and cope with the adverse impacts of disasters;  
⮚ Increase the capacity of institutions for risk governance to avert loss of lives and assets;  
⮚ Strengthen partnership among all key actors and stakeholders; and  
⮚ Develop and implement comprehensive and mutually-reinforcing national and local disaster preparedness and response plans and systems.

## Thematic Pillar III: Disaster Response and Early Recovery

## GOAL:

➢ Provide risk-based, timely and anticipatory response actions to address basic, life preservation and immediate needs of communities and government. Also, affected communities/ populations are able to continue life with dignity and prevent or minimize exacerbation of emergency situations.

## OBJECTIVES:

⮚ To activate emergency operations center equipped with response workforce and volunteers;  
⮚ To activate risk and forecast-based financing to forecasted affected communities  
⮚ To evacuate safely, preemptively and immediately the affected communities and ensure their safety  
⮚ To ensure the timely, effective and well-coordinated response action and humanitarian logistics among cluster members and other actors;  
⮚ To ensure adequate, prompt and well-coordinated assessment of needs and damages  
⮚ To immediately and temporarily restore basic needs; and  
⮚ To establish and implement an integrated system for early recovery.

## Thematic Pillar IV: Rehabilitation and Recovery

## GOAL:

<sup>➢</sup> Speed up recovery from disaster losses through rehabilitation and recovery programs that are aligned to sustainable development and “build back better” principle.

## OBJECTIVES:

⮚ Assess damages, losses, and damage needs during disasters as basis for the formulation of rehabilitation and recovery program; and  
⮚ Develop short- and medium-term rehabilitation and recovery plans, aligned with or contributing to the national medium- and long-term national, regional, or local development plans

## Local Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Plan (LDRRMP)

54. As part of the OCD’s mandate to capacitate the LGUs, particularly the provinces, cities, and municipalities, it established the Gawad Kalasag (GK) Search for Excellence in DRRM and Humanitarian Assistance, which served as the country’s recognition and awards scheme for various stakeholders who promote and implement DRRM, climate change adaptation (CCA), and humanitarian assistance programs that protect and shield high-risk communities against extreme hazards and render them more capable of addressing their vulnerabilities.  
55. The GK Seal for LDRRMCO is envisioned to serve as a performance assessment mechanism for LGUs as they implement the provisions of Sections 11 and 12 of RA No. 10121, specifically on the institutionalization of their LDRRMCs and LDRRMOs. This includes other policy issuances such as reporting on the allocation and utilization of the LDRRMF.  
56. With the assessment, the NDRRMC and LGUs can adequately determine the challenges and gaps in their LDRRMP implementation processes, which helps identify the activities to address the DRRM-CCA needs and priorities of LDRRM officers and staff, as well as LDRRMC members.  
57. The LGUs conducted a self-assessment using the LDRRMCO Assessment Toolkit. The toolkit provided a set of assessments that allowed the LGUs to assess their disaster resilience, aligning it with the LDRRMCO mandates under RA No. 10121, the Seal of Good Local Governance-Disaster Preparedness Area, and the GK indicators.  
58. Accordingly, the OCD Regional Offices (ROs) conducted field and remote validation of the LGUs' submitted Means of Verification (MoV) in accordance with NDRRMC Memorandum Circular No. 02, s. 2023 or the Guidelines for GK Seal and Special Awards for Excellence in DRRM and Humanitarian Assistance.  
59. The following are the major indicators of the LDRRMCO Assessment Toolkit:  
I. Structure - Institutional arrangement for functional DRRM.  
II. Competency - Demonstrated competencies and/or exhibited knowledge across the four thematic areas.  
III. Management Systems - Established mechanism for planning, budgeting, monitoring, and evaluation of DRRM.  
IV. Enabling Policies - Passage of relevant local DRRM and climate-related local ordinances or resolutions.  
V. Knowledge Management and Advocacy - Knowledge management platforms and information campaigns on hazards and risks.

VI. Partnership and Participation - Established and utilized citizen engagement platforms and mobilized networks and linkages for DRRM.

60. For CY 2024, 1,573 out of 1,716 provinces/cities/municipalities in the Philippines have submitted their self-assessment toolkit and joined the GK Seal CY 2024. 143 LGUs (8.30 percent) did not comply with the self-assessment toolkit.  
61. The detailed results of the assessment are presented below:

<table><tr><td colspan="2">I. STRUCTURE</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">A. Establish and Functional LDRRMC</td></tr><tr><td>Indicators</td><td>Accomplishments (out of 1,573 LGUs)</td></tr><tr><td>Establishment of LDRRMC</td><td>923 LGUs are "Beyond Compliant" and 259 are "Fully Compliant." This robust performance stems from LGUs establishing their Local DRRM Councils (LDRRMCs) through official means (Ordinance, Resolution, or Executive Order) and rigorously adhering to RA No. 10121's compositional requirements. This includes the essential representation of at least four accredited Civil Society Organizations (CSOs) and one accredited Private Sector entity. The observed increase signifies a deepening commitment by LGUs to integrate diverse offices, stakeholders, and CSOs into local planning, which is vital for cultivating safe, secure, and resilient Filipino communities.</td></tr><tr><td>Convene the LDRRMC quarterly or as necessary</td><td>664 LGUs conducted one regular quarterly meeting with proper documentation (minutes and attendance). Furthermore, 786 LGUs (50.0 percent) surpassed this requirement by holding more than one regular council meeting per quarter. Conversely, LGUs classified as "partial" or "non-compliant" either conducted fewer than the mandated annual meetings or lacked sufficient documentation for their quarterly council meetings.</td></tr><tr><td>Organization of DRRMC</td><td>982 have achieved 100 percent establishment of required subordinates, and 274 have established 75-99 percent. The increased number of partially and non-compliant LGUs is largely due to the elevated "Beyond Compliant" (80 percent to 100 percent) and "Fully Compliant" (50 percent to 75 percent) criteria. Additionally, inadequate documentation of subordinate LGU LDRRMC establishment contributed to these classifications.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">B. Creation of LDRRMO</td></tr><tr><td>Secretariat and Executive Arm of LDRRMC</td><td>875 LGUs have successfully maintained complete databases or records of all mandated quarterly full council meeting proceedings, along with documentation of adopted policies, guidelines, protocols, systems, or plans from the past three years. This demonstrates their effective provision of secretariat support to the LDRRMC and their consistent organization of regular LDRRMC meetings, ensuring all documentary requirements are met.Conversely, 244 LGUs have secured their meeting records but lack adopted policies from the past three years. The overall increase in LGUs achieving a "Beyond Compliant" rating underscores the strong commitment of LDRRMCs to convene their members and successfully endorse and adopt their DRRM initiatives and policies.</td></tr><tr><td>Indicators</td><td>Accomplishments (out of 1,573 LGUs)</td></tr><tr><td>Creation of LDRRMC Office, and LDRRMO Staffing</td><td>639 LGUs have not only established but also institutionalized their LDRRMOs in accordance with Section 12 of RA 10121, utilizing appropriate legal instruments (Ordinance, Resolution, or Executive Order). These LGUs demonstrate full adherence to the NDRRMC-DILG-DBM-CSC JMC 2014-1, even extending to the provision of an insured, separate building or office. Furthermore, 598 LGUs have met the prescribed establishment standards, and 245 LGUs have set up their LDRRMOs with at least the necessary facilities to provide services. Crucially, the percentage of LGUs without an established local DRRM office has notably decreased from 11.9 percent to 5.8 percent, indicating substantial progress.531 LGUs have surpassed the staffing requirements of NDRRMC-DILG-DBM-CSC JMC 2014-1, creating dedicated sections for research and planning, administration and training, and operations and warning. These LGUs have also upgraded their LDRRMOs to department level, in compliance with item 5.6 of the circular. Additionally, 486 LGUs have met the essential staffing mandate (permanent LDRRMO Head and three permanent staff), while 679 LGUs maintain a designated LDRRMO Head with supporting office personnel.</td></tr><tr><td>Local DRRM Officer</td><td>760 LGUs have LDRRM Officers who exceed the minimum qualifications and standards outlined in NDRRMC-DILG-DBM-CSC JMC 2014-1, possessing more training, educational attainment, and eligibility requirements than the minimum requirements. Additionally, 524 LGUs have LDRRM Officers who meet the required qualifications, while 193 have an LDRRM Officer either appointed or serving in a concurrent capacity.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">C. Establish Local DRRM Operation Center</td></tr><tr><td>Establishment of Provincial/City/Municipal DRRRM Operations Center</td><td>531 LGUs have successfully set up the prescribed 24/7 OpCen and designated alternate Emergency Operations Centers.Moreover, 782 LGUs maintain an active operations center meeting required operational standards. Further, 206 LGUs activate their OpCen during disaster events.</td></tr><tr><td>Organization and Competence of local Emergency Response Teams (ERTs)</td><td>771 LGUs (49 percent) organized Emergency Response Teams (ERTs) compared to last year (34.0 percent). This growth indicates that LGUs are widely exceeding the basic requirements for ERT organization and competence. This includes having response equipment for more than their top two hazards, a surplus of ERTs trained in SRR and Basic ICS, insured ERT personnel, and fully organized equipment inventories.Moreover, 535 LGUs have met the core requirements for trained ERTs and hazard-specific response assets. A further 212 LGUs possess at least personnel equipped for Civil Defense and DRRM duties</td></tr></table>

II. COMPETENCY

A. Capacitated on Disaster Prevention and Mitigation

<table><tr><td>Indicators</td><td>Accomplishments</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Risk Assessment</td></tr><tr><td>Facilitation and Support to Risk Assessment</td><td>554 LGUs conducted risk assessments and successfully incorporated them into DRRM/CCA-related plans using updated tools such asCDRA, REDAS, or GeoRiskPH platforms. These assessments produced output risk maps with the active participation of vulnerable sectors.Additionally, 359 LGUs conducted risk assessments using CDRA, REDAS, or GeoRiskPH platforms. Meanwhile, 527 LGUs utilized other LGU-led or sponsored tools for their assessments. Notably, there has been a significant decrease in the number of LGUs that have not conducted risk assessments, dropping from 24.4 percent to 8.5 percent (133 LGUs). This reduction signifies LGUs' increasing acknowledgment of the importance of risk assessment in their local planning.</td></tr><tr><td>Maintenance of Local Risk Maps</td><td>683 LGUs maintain an updated inventory of these maps, which are reproduced and displayed for more than their top two local hazards. This increase from last year's 31.4 percent signifies the growing recognition of the importance of risk maps in planning, raising awareness of hazards and risks, and facilitating the dissemination of information. Meanwhile, 406 LGUs and 364 LGUs have local risk maps for at least one hazard in their respective localities.</td></tr><tr><td>Early Warning System (EWS)</td><td>516 LGUs have an approved and adopted Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) or Manual for a laymanized and localized Multi-hazard EWS. These systems are fully compliant with the four elements of an EWS: Risk Knowledge, Monitoring and Warning Service, Dissemination and Communication, and Response Capability, and also incorporate traditional or indigenous monitoring methods. Meanwhile, 608 LGUs have a draft EWS SOP or Manual, and 367 LGUs possess an available EWS in place.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">B. Capacitated on Disaster Preparedness</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Planning</td></tr><tr><td>Designing, Programming, and Coordination of DRRM ActivitiesPresence of approved and adopted LDRRM Plan</td><td>942 LGUs have updated, LDRRMC-approved, and Sanggunian-adopted LDRRMPs—all certified by the reviewing authority for compliance with NDRRMC Memorandum No. 147 s.2017. Furthermore, 325 LGUs have approved and adopted the LDRRMP with pending compliance as a result of review, while 219 LGUs have approved and adopted the LDRRMP that have not yet been reviewed by the reviewing authority.Concurrently, a positive trend is the substantial reduction in LGUs with only draft LDRRMPs, plummeting from 11.3 percent to 5.5 percent (87 LGUs) over the assessment period. This highlights the growing capacity of LGUs to finalize LDRRMPs that effectively articulate goals and specific objectives for disaster risk reduction, along with actionable strategies to achieve them.</td></tr><tr><td>Presence of DRRM Plans</td><td>954 LGUs continue to ensure their component LGUs or barangays have adopted DRRM Plans.Moreover, 256 LGUs maintain at least 75 percent of their lower LGUs with approved plans, and 85 LGUs have at least 50 percent. A significant reduction in LGUs with less than 50 percent lower LGU plan coverage (from 26.9 percent to 17.7 percent or 278 LGUs) further</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>solidifies LGUs' dedication to empowering their component units in DRRM Planning.</td></tr><tr><td>Presence of LCCAP and Climate Change (CC) expenditure tagged AIP</td><td>944 LGUs have integrated their LCCAPs into their CLUP or CDP and submitted them to the Climate Change Commission. Additionally, 338 LGUs have an approved LCCAP, and 141 LGUs have a draft.</td></tr><tr><td>Presence of Contingency Plans (CP)</td><td>943 LGUs have approved and adopted updated Contingency Plans (CPs) that include provisions for COVID/Emerging Infectious Diseases (EREID) for at least two identified hazards within the last three years. The rise from the previous year indicates that more LGUs are proactively planning for projected natural and/or human-induced hazards to address their impacts and/or prevent the occurrence of emerging threats.Furthermore, 285 LGUs have CPs for at least two hazards, while 166 LGUs have CPs for at least one hazard in their locality. Notably, there has been a decline from 21.3 percent to 11.4 percent (179 LGUs) in LGUs without any approved CPs, signaling positive progress.</td></tr><tr><td>Monitor and evaluate the implementation of the LDRRMPs and regularly review and test the plan consistent with other national and local planning program</td><td>1,250 LGUS possess approved Local DRRM Plans (LDRRMPs) and accompanying Monitoring &amp; Evaluation (M&amp;E) Reports.Within this group, reporting frequencies vary: 596 LGUs provide quarterly reports, 174 LGUs submit semestral reports, and 480 LGUs provide annual reports. Despite this progress, a substantial gap remains, with 323 LGUs lacking any proper M&amp;E reporting mechanism.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Capacity-Building</td></tr><tr><td>Organization and Conduct of Training (including Training of Trainers), Orientation and Knowledge Management Activities on DRRM at the Local Level</td><td>495 LGUs have developed internal pools of trainers through "training of trainers" programs. Additionally, 545 LGUs have organized and conducted at least five DRRM-related trainings or workshops.Meanwhile, 371 LGUs have conducted fewer than five DRRM-related activities. A notable decrease which is 162 LGUs in providing proper documentation for their DRRM-related activities, hindering verification.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">C. Capacitated on Disaster Response</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Evacuation</td></tr><tr><td>Recommend and implementation of forced or pre-emptive evacuation of local residents, if necessary</td><td>899 LGUs have updated SOPs and have successfully enforced evacuations with zero (0) casualties, supported by official issuances (ordinance, resolution, or executive order).Another 460 LGUs meet pre-emptive evacuation standards, including SOP presence. Significantly, the decline in partially (22.7 percent to 10.2 percent) and non-compliant (7.1 percent to 3.4 percent) LGUs underscores their growing readiness and responsiveness in protecting lives during disasters.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Camp Coordination and Camp Management</td></tr><tr><td>Pre-positioning</td><td>737 LGUs have a properly managed stockpile of relief goods and standby arrangements through MOU or notarized MOA with the local suppliers. While 461 LGUs have available stockpiles of relief goods and standby arrangements and 250 LGUs have standby arrangementsthrough MOU or notarized MOA with suppliers. In spite of that, there are still 125 LGUs that have no proper stockpile of relief goods nor standby arrangements through MOU or MOA with the local suppliers.</td></tr><tr><td>Camp Coordination and Camp Management</td><td>303 LGUs have fully complied with these requirements. These LGUs can provide documentation for a functional space (Child-Friendly Space/Women-Friendly Space) equipped with available CCCM kits, CFS kits, and WFS kits. They also ensure the availability of Information Boards and Referral Pathways.In addition, 530 LGUs met the minimum requirements, while 502 LGUs have at least a draft Camp Management Plan and its organizational structure. Notably, there's a significant decrease in LGUs lacking proper documentation on camp management, falling from 33.4 percent to 15.1 percent.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">Operations</td></tr><tr><td>Response to and Management of Adverse effects of Emergencies/Disasters</td><td>602 LGUs demonstrate the full capacity to respond to and manage adverse effects under both normal and alert conditions. A slightly smaller group of 562 LGUs have achieved the presence of organized emergency response teams, robust systems, clear procedures, established protocols, and proven timely and inclusive response operations during both normal and alert phases.However, 281 LGUs have only managed to establish organized ERTs, systems, procedures, and protocols. Critically, 128 LGUs remain without any organized teams, systems, or protocols, indicating a significant gap in their response capabilities.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">D. Capacitated on Disaster Rehabilitation and Recovery</td></tr><tr><td>Facilitation of early recovery and rehabilitation interventions</td><td>1,376 LGUs have rehabilitation and recovery programs, projects, and activities within their Local DRRM Plan or RRP implementation. Of these, 506 LGUs actively monitor their implementation. Another 467 LGUs have implemented these strategies and programs, and 403 LGUs have them in their LDRRMP. Critically, however, 197 LGUs still lack organized rehabilitation interventions or activities.</td></tr><tr><td>Formulation of rehabilitation and recovery program</td><td>1,309 LGUs possess databases containing socioeconomic and disaster-related statistics and maps, lists of identified critical areas, assets, and population centers, as well as Local DRRM Fund and GSIS property insurance information.Of these, 309 LGUs effectively manage proper databases for the aforementioned information and can provide evidence of other disaster risk financing and insurance mechanisms. An additional 522 LGUs have provided lists of identified critical areas, assets, and population centers. Meanwhile, 478 LGUs maintain a basic database of information. However, 264 LGUs show lacking evidence in their pre-disaster planning documentation.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">III. MANAGEMENT SYSTEMS</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">A. Established Standard Operating Procedures</td></tr><tr><td>Indicators</td><td>Accomplishments</td></tr><tr><td>Hazard-specific SOPs</td><td>415 LGUs have tested SOPs for over their top two hazards, while 668 LGUs have tested them for their top two hazards. Additionally, 355LGUs have tested SOPs for at least one hazard. Despite this progress, a notable gap persists: 135 LGUs have yet to establish SOPs or provide adequate documentation.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">B. Mainstreaming of DRRM and CCA in Local Development Plans, Program, and Budgeting</td></tr><tr><td>Ensure the integration of Risk Reduction and Climate Change adaptation into local development plans, programs, and budgets as a strategy for sustainable development and poverty reduction</td><td>1,387 LGUs have a Risk-Informed CLUP and CDP. Of these, 427 LGUs have approved and adopted CLUPs; 685 LGUs have either approved or adopted CLUPs; and 275 LGUs have draft CLUPs. Conversely, 186 LGUs lack a Risk-Informed CLUP and CDP.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">C. Local DRRM Funding Utilization</td></tr><tr><td>Programming and budgeting for LDRRMF and other DRRM resources/funding and budgetary support to LDRMMO</td><td>1,289 LGUs have programming and budgeting of Local DRRM Funds and other related resources/funding and budgetary support for Local DRRM Offices and Councils (LDRRMCOs). Particularly, 101 LGUs achieved a 95 to 100 percent utilization rate, 526 LGUs demonstrated a utilization rate not lower than 75 percent, and 662 LGUs had a 50 to 75 percent utilization rate. However, 284 LGUs recorded a utilization rate below 50 percent.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">IV. ENABLING POLICIES</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">A. LDRRM Council Recommended DRRM-Related Policies</td></tr><tr><td>Indicators</td><td>Accomplishments</td></tr><tr><td>Recommendation to enact DRRM-related ordinances through LDRRMC</td><td>722 have seen their proposed DRRM-related legislation, environmental codes, and ordinances or policy recommendations gain approval and adoption by the Sanggunian. Furthermore, 339 LGUs were successful in proposing new legislations or ordinances, while 314 LGUs offered significant support to the Sanggunian's legislative efforts.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">V. KNOWLEDGE MANAGEMENT AND ADVOCACY</td></tr><tr><td>Local Disaster Database Resource</td><td>1,440 LGUs maintain a database covering their DRR-related resources, permanent evacuation centers, capacity, and inventory maps. Of these, 474 LGUs possess complete and disaggregated databases, while 520 LGUs have successfully complied with the required database standards. Meanwhile, 446 LGUs maintain a simple database of information. However, 133 LGUs have not been able to properly document or database their information.</td></tr><tr><td>Conducted information dissemination and public awareness</td><td>1,494 LGUs maintain an existing IEC program. Within this group, 796 LGUs stand out by developing and disseminating IEC materials that are contextualized, laymanized, and presented in popular language across diverse media platforms. Furthermore, 428 LGUs have advanced their dissemination efforts by basing them on risk assessment outcomes and strategic communication plans, or by innovating with effective, practical, and sustainable traditional or tech-based approaches.However, a segment of 270 LGUs possesses only an existing IEC program, and a concerning 79 LGUs currently lack any IEC program.</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">VI. PARTNERSHIP AND PARTICIPATION</td></tr><tr><td>Develop, strengthen and operationalize mechanisms for partnership or networking with the private sector, CSOs and volunteer groups</td><td>1,418 LGUs have mechanisms in place for collaboration with the private sector, CSOs, and volunteer groups, covering essential services like relief distribution, medical aid, psycho-social support, and security.Within this group, 441 LGUs have comprehensive partnerships with NGOs spanning all four thematic areas. Additionally, 556 LGUs engaged NGOs in at least two of the thematic areas, and 421 LGUs had partnerships in at least one thematic area. While 155 LGUs reported no partnerships, this represents a significant improvement from the previous year's 16.8 percent.</td></tr><tr><td>Participation in other external activities set by higher DRRM Councils</td><td>1,062 LGUs conducted five or more identified activities within a year. These activities include the Nationwide Simultaneous Earthquake Drill, National Disaster Resilience Month-related activities, Gawad KALASAG, DRRM-related training, DRRM-related seminars or forums, and other stakeholder engagements.Accordingly, 231 LGUs managed more than three activities within the assessment year, and 175 LGUs participated in one to two (1-2) identified activities. Despite these efforts, 105 LGUs had no participation in external activities set by higher DRRM Councils. The decrease in LGUs categorized as "Fully" to "Non-Compliant" is attributed to the increasing number of LGUs exceeding the required level of DRRM-related participation.</td></tr></table>

## IV. Consolidated Audit Observations and Recommendations

62. The consolidated significant audit observations and recommendations herein summarized were lifted from the AARs<sup>14</sup> of 10 NGAs, 6 GOCCs, along with the region-wide consolidated significant audit observations and recommendations of 877 LGUs (54 provinces, 84 cities and 739 municipalities) submitted by the OAC-LGAS, of this Commission. The details are shown in Annex B.

## NDRRM FUNDS – NGAs and GOCCs

## A. Implementation of NDRRM-related PAPs

Deficiencies in the implementation of various DRRM programs; hence, benefits were not maximized or the objectives were not attained  
63. The slow implementation of various DRRM projects defeats the purpose of RA No. 10121 or the Philippine Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Act of 2010, which intends to lessen the impact of disasters and facilitate the resumption of normal social and economic activities.

63.1 Section 3 of RA No. 10121 provides that it is the policy of the State to uphold the people’s constitutional right to life and property by addressing the root causes of vulnerabilities to disasters, strengthening the country’s institutional capacity for DRRM, and building the resilience of local communities to disasters, including climate change impacts.  
63.2 Relative thereto, various PAPs were initiated by different government agencies in pursuit of their mandates. However, the audit noted that some projects remained unimplemented or, if implemented, were not completed within the target dates, thereby depriving the intended beneficiaries of the benefits that could have been derived from them. The details are shown in Table 13:

Table 13: Delayed Projects by Various Government Agencies

<table><tr><td>Agency</td><td>Program</td><td>Observation</td></tr><tr><td>DA</td><td>DRRM and QRF</td><td>Low fund utilizationDeficiencies such as: (a) low fund utilization and disbursements; (b) undocumented and undisclosed credit procurements of DRRM Inventory and PPE Items; and (c) insufficient documents for disbursements of the QRF/Calamity Fund for CY 2024 in three DA offices (RFOs IV-A, IV-B, V) were observed due to delayed receipt of funds from the Central Office and lacking supporting documents,thus, deprives the calamity-affected constituents of immediate benefits from the fund and reflects inadequacies in the implementation process.This is due to delayed receipt of funds from the Central Office and inadequacies in the implementation process, which may deprive the calamity-affected constituents of immediate benefits from the fund.Also, there were lacking documents to support the disbursement, which precluded the Audit Team from verifying the regularity of the claims and transactions.</td></tr><tr><td>2. DHSUD</td><td>DRRM</td><td>Deficiencies in the distribution of financial assistance in CARThe Team noted lapses in the distribution to the beneficiaries of financial assistance for households with totally and partially damaged houses due to the 2022 Magnitude 7.0 Earthquake in Northwestern Luzon contrary to pertinent provisions of COA Circular 2021-014 and DHSUD-CAR RMC No. 101, s 2024, thus, casting doubt on the validity of claims, transparency and accountability over the public funds and timeliness of the distribution.This is due to the late submission by the concerned LGUs, inadequate planning in the mode of distribution, and lack of manpower in the agency, which delayed the distribution of the financial assistance, resulting in the reversion of the funds to the National Treasury.</td></tr><tr><td>3. DOH</td><td>DRRM</td><td>Deficiencies noted in the management of fundsThe purchase of groceries of ₱162,817.12, athletic uniforms for cultural and athletic activities for the DOH commemorative event (5th Founding Anniversary) and payments for fuel used in the operations of ambulances totaling ₱574,710.00 charged against the DRRM fund was not aligned with the activities outlined under Section IV, Items 3 and 5 of DOH DO No. 2024-0047 and DOH AO No. 2012-003.Additionally, the buffer stock level for emergency supplies is below the required level prescribed under DOH AO No. 2012-0013.</td></tr><tr><td>4. DPWH</td><td>NDRRMF</td><td>The NDRRMF allocated to DPWH-OSEC, totaling ₱187.10 million remained unutilized as of December 31, 2024, due to lack of planning, coordination, and effective fund managementThe allotments received pertained solely to the Engineering and Administrative Head (EAO) share of the Central Office(CO), which are earmarked for specific purposes, as indicated in the SARO, thus limiting their flexibility in utilization.The CO’s share of the EAO is automatic, based on the enforcement of DPWH DO No. 05, series of 2024, under Section 18 of the Specific Provisions of the CY 2024 GAA.This order authorized the Department to deduct the EAO from every infrastructure project costing over ₱1 million.</td></tr><tr><td>5. LWUA</td><td>DRRM</td><td>Delays in the completion of DRRM projectsMarawi CityThe completion of the rehabilitation and reactivation of the Marawi City Water District (MCWD) Project was already delayed by five years due to poor project implementation and inadequate supervision by LWUA, thus, casting doubt on the reported 92 percent project completion, which is not in accordance with COA Circular No. 94-013 and LWUA Office Order No. 154.19The following were noted by the Audit Team:absence of Monthly Project Payment Reports (MPPR) and Actual Work Accomplishment Reports (AWAR) on project milestones; andnon-submission of utilization reports during the 5-year implementation of the projectCagayan de Oro CityThe release of subsidy funds amounting to ₱63.62 million to the Cagayan de Oro City Water District (COWD) for the construction and rehabilitation of water supply systems in areas affected by Typhoon Sendong was not efficiently utilized due to prolonged project completion, which was attributed to inadequate monitoring and supervision, and the absence of a specific timeline for implementation in the Memorandum of Agreement (MOA), contrary to Section 2(i) and (p) of RA No. 10121 and Section 75 of PD No. 198, as amended by PD No. 768.</td></tr><tr><td>6. NHA</td><td>Yolanda Permanent Housing Program (YPHP)</td><td>Only 174,483 housing units (HUs), or 89.45 percent of the revised target of 195,062 HUs, have been completedEleven years after the launch of the YPHP following Typhoon Yolanda, only 174,483 housing units (HUs) equivalent to 89.45 per cent of the revised target of 195,062 have been completed due largely to project suspensions, terminations, budgetary constraints, and realignments, preventing many typhoon victims from receiving the homes they were promised.Moreover, poor transportation access, limited opportunities for livelihood, inadequate water and electricity services, and the use of inferior building materials have all contributed to the project's low occupancy rate, resulting in the rapid deterioration of the housing units.Additionally, several factors contributed to the delay in completing the 128 projects within the target timeframe. These include suspension orders, extensions due to bad weather, changes in housing design requested by LGUs, minor corrections, ongoing contract termination processes, land surveying and titling, power line installation, adjustments in scope due to unbuildable areas, and disputes with land claimants.As a result, the intended beneficiaries of these delayed, suspended and terminated projects have been deprived of their immediate use. Moreover, delays in implementing these housing projects may undermine the agency's credibility in disaster response and erode public trust in its ability to provide timely and adequate housing assistance to those affected by calamities, especially considering that 11 years have already elapsed.</td></tr><tr><td>7. PCG</td><td>2023NDRRMF:Response operations due to the oil spill incident in IV-B and Region VI</td><td>Allotments totaling ₱314.28 million or 91.77 percent of the total NDRRMF of ₱342.47 million released in CY 2023 remained unutilizedThe PCG initiated the procurement of several Marine Environmental Protection-related projects; however, it was discovered that it was not aligned with the intended purpose outlined in the SARO. Consequently, they coordinated with the NDRRMC/OCD to determine if it was possible to procure rubber boats instead.The PCG subsequently requested an Authority to Purchase Motor Vehicle from the Office of the President. Still, the request was denied, and the decision was communicated in the last quarter of CY 2024. Hence, there was insufficient time to proceed with the procurement activities before the end of the fiscal year.</td></tr></table>

63.3. The slow implementation of DRRM projects resulted in delays in the completion of said projects and defeated the purpose of allocating funds to provide an immediate response to the intended beneficiaries. Furthermore, it is not consistent with the state's policy to mitigate the impact of calamities and assist communities affected by natural and human-induced disasters in rebuilding and returning to their normal lives.

63.4. The Audit Teams recommended the following:

<table><tr><td>Agency</td><td>Recommendations</td></tr><tr><td>1. DA</td><td>Concerned units and personnel in:a. RFO IV-A to coordinate with the Rehabilitation and Recovery Support Office and promptly submit the requests for the release of QRF in the event of a State of Calamity, duly supported with all required documents;c. RFO V to:submit the lacking documents for the ₱188 million already obligated in CY 2024 and for the ₱1.29 million ASF indemnification paid to swine farmers affected by ASF in July 2024, to prove the validity of claims and transactions;fast-track the provision of various assistance to beneficiaries affected by Typhoon Kristine; andreturn to the BTr any unexpended/unutilized funds or savings, if warranted.</td></tr><tr><td>2.
